Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer base b>9, where this means digits possible 0-…(b-1). 17_b is a divisor (exactly divides) 97_b. We need to express numbers in base 10: 17_b = 1*b +7 = b+7. 97_b = 9*b +7. We want (b+7) divides (9b+7). Let d = b+7. Then 9b+7 = 9(b) +7. Express in terms of d: b = d -7. Then 9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d -63 +7 = 9d -56. Want d divides 9d -56. Equivalent to d divides 56? Because 9d -56 ≡ -56 (mod d), so d | 56. So d must be a positive divisor of 56 such that d = b+7 >? Also base >9, so b>9, hence d = b+7>16. Also digits in base: 9 is a digit allowed? In base b>9, digits 0- (b-1) include 9 if b>10 actually? Wait base > 9 means b≥10. For base 10, digit 9 is allowed. For base 9, digit 9 not allowed. So base must be >9. So b≥10. That's fine.

Now d = b+7 will be at least 10+7 =17. So d > 16.

Also note that b must also satisfy b>9 and 9 must be a legal digit, meaning b>9 ensures digit 9 allowed. Also 7 digit obviously allowed if b>7? B≥10 ensures both digits allowed. So fine.

So we need positive integer divisors d of 56 that are >16. Let's compute divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Those >16: 28 and 56. So potential d = 28 or 56. Then b = d -7. For d=28, b = 21. b>9? Yes. For d=56, b = 49. b>9? yes.

But we must also check that the base digits used (9 and 7) are valid: for base 21 digits 0-20, includes 9 and 7, yes. For base 49 digits 0-48, includes 9 and 7, yes. Also condition b>9 satisfied. Also we should check that 17_b is divisor of 97_b. Let's confirm: For b=21: 17_b = 21+7=28. 97_b = 9*21+7 = 189+7 = 196. Does 28 divide 196? 28*7=196. Yes.

For b=49: 17_b=49+7=56. 97_b=9*49+7=441+7=448. 56 divides 448? 56*8=448. Yes.

Thus both bases work. Sum of all integer bases >9: 21+49=70. So answer: 70.

But let's double-check if there are other possible d: 56 factors 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Only 28 and 56 >16. Good.

assistant 
To work in base \(b\) we translate the numbers to base \(10\).

\[
17_b = 1\cdot b+7 = b+7,\qquad 
97_b = 9\cdot b+7 = 9b+7.
\]

We need \(b+7\) to divide \(9b+7\).

Let \(d=b+7\). Then \(b=d-7\) and

\[
9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d-56.
\]

Hence, \(d \mid 9d-56 \iff d \mid 56\).

Thus, \(d\) must be a positive divisor of \(56\).  
The divisors of \(56\) are \(1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56\).

Because \(b>9\), we have \(d=b+7>16\).  
The only divisors of \(56\) larger than \(16\) are \(28\) and \(56\).

* \(d=28\): \(b=28-7=21\).
  \[
  17_{21}=28,\quad 97_{21}=9\cdot21+7=196,\quad 28\mid196.
  \]
* \(d=56\): \(b=56-7=49\).
  \[
  17_{49}=56,\quad 97_{49}=9\cdot49+7=448,\quad 56\mid448.
  \]

Both bases satisfy the condition.  
The sum of all required integer bases is

\[
21+49=70.
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Correct Answer: 70

assistant 
Let  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(28,0)
\]

Place \(C=(x,y)\).  
Because \(C\) lies on the line \(AC\), the points on \(AC\) are

\[
F=\Bigl(\frac{13}{91}x,\;\frac{13}{91}y\Bigr), \qquad  
G=\Bigl(\frac{65}{91}x,\;\frac{65}{91}y\Bigr).
\]

The points on \(AB\) are  

\[
D=(4,0), \qquad
E=(20,0).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Height of the triangle

The area of \(DEGF\) is given to be \(288\).  
Using the shoelace formula for the polygon  
\(D(4,0),E(20,0),G,\;F\) gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
2[DEGF]
   &=|4(0)+20\,\frac{65}{91}y
     +\frac{65}{91}x\cdot 6
     +\frac{13}{91}x\cdot0   \\
   &\quad-\;0\cdot20
     -0\cdot\frac{65}{91}x
     -\frac{13}{91}x\cdot\frac{65}{91}y
     -6\cdot4 |\\
   &=\frac{48}{7}\,y .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence  

\[
\frac12\Bigl(\frac{48}{7}y\Bigr)=288	\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
y=42 .
\]

So the height of \(\triangle ABC\) above \(AB\) is \(y=42\).

The area of \(\triangle ABC\) is

\[
[ABC]=\frac12\;AB\cdot y=\frac12(28)(42)=14\cdot 42=588 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The reflected points

The reflections are

\[
M=\text{reflection of }D\text{ through }F
   =2F-D
   =\Bigl(\frac{26}{91}x-4,\;12\Bigr),
\]

\[
N=\text{reflection of }G\text{ through }E
   =2E-G
   =\Bigl(40-\frac{65}{91}x,\;-30\Bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

Using the shoelace formula for the polygon  

\[
A(0,0),\;F,\;N,\;B(28,0),\;C,\;E,\;M,
\]

we obtain

\[
\begin{aligned}
2[AFNBCEM]
 &=|0\cdot \tfrac{13}{91}y
   +\tfrac{13}{91}x\cdot(-30)
   +\bigl(40-\tfrac{65}{91}x\bigr) \cdot0   \\
 &\quad +28\,y
   -0\cdot 28
   -0\cdot x
   -\frac{65}{91}x \cdot0
   -x( -30) \\
 &=28\,y .
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore  

\[
[AFNBCEM]=\frac12(28y)=14y .
\]

With \(y=42\),

\[
[AFNBCEM]=14\cdot 42=588 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{588}
\]

assistant 
\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\]

Factor the quadratic (it is homogeneous):

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=(4x-3y)(3x+2y)
\]

Hence we need  

\[
(4x-3y)(3x+2y)=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\begin{cases}
4x-3y=0,\\[2pt]
\text{or}\\[2pt]
3x+2y=0 .
\end{cases}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  \(4x-3y=0\)

\(4x=3y \;\Rightarrow\; y=\dfrac{4}{3}x\)

For \(y\) to be integer, \(x\) must be a multiple of \(3\).  
Let \(x=3k\) (\(k\in \mathbb Z\)); then \(y=4k\).

Both coordinates must lie between \(-100\) and \(100\):

\[
|3k|\le 100 \;\Rightarrow\; |k|\le 33,\qquad
|4k|\le 100 \;\Rightarrow\; |k|\le 25.
\]

Thus \(|k|\le 25\), giving  

\[
k=-25,-24,\dots ,0,\dots ,24,25
\]

— \(51\) integer values, hence \(51\) ordered pairs \((3k,4k)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  \(3x+2y=0\)

\(3x=-2y \;\Rightarrow\; y=-\dfrac{3}{2}x\)

Here \(x\) must be an even integer. Let \(x=2k\); then \(y=-3k\).

Bounding each coordinate:

\[
|2k|\le 100 \;\Rightarrow\; |k|\le 50,\qquad
|\,3k\,|\le 100 \;\Rightarrow\; |k|\le 33.
\]

Hence \(|k|\le 33\), giving \(67\) integer values of \(k\)
and \(67\) ordered pairs \((2k,-3k)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting distinct solutions

Both families contain the pair \((0,0)\).  
All other pairs are distinct (the equations \(y=4k\) and \(y=-3k\)
cannot coincide for a non‑zero \(k\)).

Total distinct solutions:

\[
51\;(\text{first family}) + 67\;(\text{second family}) - 1\;(\text{duplicate}) = 117.
\]

\[
\boxed{117}

Let the 8-digit integer be \(a_1a_2a_3a_4a_5a_6a_7a_8\).

*Divisibility by \(2\):*  
The last digit \(a_8\) must be even, so \(a_8\in\{2,4,6,8\}\).

*Divisibility by \(11\):*  
For an 8‑digit number,
\[
(a_1+a_3+a_5+a_7)-(a_2+a_4+a_6+a_8)\equiv 0\pmod{11}.
\]
Let \(S_{\text{odd}}\) be the sum of the digits in positions \(1,3,5,7\).
Since the total of the digits \(1+2+\ldots+8=36\),
\[
S_{\text{even}}=36-S_{\text{odd}},
\]
and
\[
2S_{\text{odd}}-36\equiv0\pmod{11}\Longrightarrow 2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 36\equiv3\pmod{11}.
\]
Multiplying by the inverse of \(2\) modulo \(11\) (which is \(6\)) gives
\[
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3\cdot6\equiv 7 \pmod{11}.
\]
With \(S_{\text{odd}}\) an integer between 10 and 26, the only possibility is
\[
S_{\text{odd}}=18,\qquad S_{\text{even}}=18.
\]

Thus the four digits occupying the odd positions must sum to 18.

---

### Counting the numbers

1. Choose the last (even) digit: \(4\) options.

2. With the last digit fixed, we have 7 remaining digits.  
   From these, pick \(4\) digits whose sum is \(18\).  
   For any of the four possible last digits there are exactly \(4\) such 4‑element subsets.

3. Arrange the chosen four digits in the odd positions (\(1,3,5,7\)): \(4!\) ways.  
   Arrange the remaining three digits in the even positions \(2,4,6\): \(3!\) ways.

Hence
\[
N = 4\ \text{(last digit choices)} \times
    4\ \text{(valid 4‑digit subsets)} \times
    4! \times 3! = 4 \times 4 \times 24 \times 6 = 2304.
\]

---

### Required difference
\[
\bigl|N-2025\bigr| = 2304-2025 = 279.
\]

\boxed{279}

Sampled message

assistant 
For a tangential quadrilateral the incircle is tangent to all four sides.  
In a trapezoid that is also isosceles we can use this property together with the
height.

---

### 1.  Height of the trapezoid  

The circle touches both parallel sides, so the centre of the circle is
equidistant from the two bases.  
Hence the distance between the bases (the height \(h\)) equals twice the
radius:

\[
h=2\cdot3=6 .
\]

---

### 2.  Relation between the bases  

Area of a trapezoid:

\[
A=\frac{(r+s)}{2}\,h .
\]

Given \(A=72\) and \(h=6\),

\[
72=\frac{(r+s)}{2}\cdot 6 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad r+s=24. \tag{1}
\]

---

### 3.  Length of the legs  

For a tangential quadrilateral the sum of the lengths of opposite sides are
equal.  
In our trapezoid the opposite sides are a base and a leg, so

\[
r+s= \text{leg}_1+\text{leg}_2 = 2\,\text{leg}.
\]

Thus the equal legs have length

\[
\text{leg}= \frac{r+s}{2}=12. \tag{2}
\]

---

### 4.  Horizontal shift between the bases  

Let the shift on each side be \(x\).  
The leg, height, and shift form a right triangle:

\[
\text{leg}^{\,2}=h^{\,2}+x^{\,2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
12^{2}=6^{2}+x^{2}\;\Longrightarrow\;x^2=108\;\Longrightarrow\;x=6\sqrt3.
\]

The difference of the bases satisfies

\[
\frac{s-r}{2}=x=6\sqrt3 \;\Longrightarrow\; s-r=12\sqrt3. \tag{3}
\]

---

### 5.  Solve for \(r\) and \(s\)

From (1) and (3):

\[
\begin{cases}
s+r = 24,\\
s-r = 12\sqrt3.
\end{cases}
\]

Adding and subtracting:

\[
\begin{aligned}
2s &= 24+12\sqrt3 \;\Longrightarrow\; s = 12+6\sqrt3,\\[2pt]
2r &= 24-12\sqrt3 \;\Longrightarrow\; r = 12-6\sqrt3.
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 6.  Compute \(r^{2}+s^{2}\)

Let \(a=12\) and \(b=6\sqrt3\).  
Then \(r=a-b,\; s=a+b\).  
Hence

\[
r^{2}+s^{2}=(a-b)^{2}+(a+b)^{2}=2(a^{2}+b^{2}).
\]

Now

\[
a^{2}=144,\qquad b^{2}=(6\sqrt3)^{2}=108,
\]
\[
a^{2}+b^{2}=144+108=252,
\]
\[
r^{2}+s^{2}=2\cdot252=504.
\]

---

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
The Twelve letters can be grouped randomly into six unordered pairs.  
In each pair the letters are written in alphabetical order, producing six
two‑letter “words”.  
These six words are then sorted alphabetically.  
We need the probability that the last (i.e. the lexicographically largest
word) contains the letter \(G\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Total number of groupings

The number of ways to partition 12 distinct letters into six unordered
pairs is  

\[
\frac{12!}{2!^{6}\,6!}=10395 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  When does the last word contain \(G\)?

Let a pair be denoted \(\{X,Y\}\) with \(X<Y\); the associated word is
\(\;XY\).  
The \(6\) words are sorted by the first letter \(X\) (alphabetical
order).  
Hence the **last word** is the one whose first letter is the
maximum of the six first letters.

Call the pair containing \(G\) the *G‑pair*.  
For the last word to contain \(G\), the first letter of the G‑pair must
be the greatest of all first letters.  
In other words,

> **The partner of \(G\) must be the maximum of the six first
> letters.**

We consider two mutually exclusive possibilities for the partner of \(G\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Case 1 –  \(G\) is the *smaller* letter of its pair  
\(G\) is paired with a letter \(Y>G\).

Then \(X=G\) is already the largest possible first letter (all other
first letters come from \(\{A,\dots ,F\}\)).  
To keep \(G\) the largest, every letter \(>G\) must be paired with a
letter \(\le G\); otherwise a pair whose first letter is \(>G\)
would appear later.

After removing the G‑pair \(\{G,Y\}\) we have

* 6 letters \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\),
* 4 remaining large letters \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\setminus\{Y\}\).

The 4 large letters each must be paired with a distinct small letter.
Choose which 4 of the 6 small letters will do so: \(\binom{6}{4}=15\) ways.  
Assign the 4 large letters to these chosen small letters: \(4! = 24\) ways.

Thus for each choice of \(Y\) the number of valid completions is

\[
\binom{6}{4}\,4! = 15\cdot24 = 360 .
\]

There are 5 possible choices for \(Y\) (\(H,I,J,K,L\)),
so

\[
N_{1}=5\cdot360 = 1800 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Case 2 –  \(G\) is the *larger* letter of its pair  
\(G\) is paired with a letter \(X<G\).

Let the G‑pair be \(\{X,G\}\).
Now \(X\) is the first letter of that pair; for the last word to contain
\(G\) we must have \(X\) the greatest of all first letters.

All letters \(>X\) (except \(G\)) must be paired with letters \(\le X\).
Write the remaining letters after removing the G‑pair:

* small letters \(\{A,\dots ,X-1\}\) – in total \(X-1\) of them,
* large letters \(\{X+1,\dots ,L\}\setminus\{G\}\) – in total \(11-X\).

To have a valid matching we need \(X-1 \ge 11-X\); otherwise there would
not be enough small letters for the large ones, and the G‑pair could not
be the last.

The only value of \(X\) satisfying this is \(X=F\) (\(X=6\)):
\[
X-1 =5,\qquad 11-X =5 .
\]

With \(X=F\) we must pair each of the five large letters
\(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\) with one of the five small letters \(\{A,B,C,D,E\}\).
There is no leftover small letter to pair among itself.  
The number of matchings is therefore the number of bijections between the
two sets:

\[
N_{2}=5! = 120 .
\]

For any other \(X\) no pairing can satisfy the required
condition, so no other contributions arise.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total favorable pairings and probability

\[
N_{\text{fav}} = N_{1}+N_{2}=1800+120=1920 .
\]

Hence

\[
P=\frac{N_{\text{fav}}}{N_{\text{total}}}=
\frac{1920}{10395}.
\]

The fraction simplifies:

\[
\gcd(1920,10395)=15,\qquad
P=\frac{1920/15}{10395/15}=\frac{128}{693}.
\]

The integers \(m=128,\;n=693\) are coprime, so

\[
m+n=128+693=821.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{821}
\]

assistant 
The equations

\[
|25+20i-z|=5 ,\qquad |z-(4+k)|=|z-(k+3i)|
\]

express that \(z\) is on a circle centered at \(25+20i\) with radius \(5\) and
that \(z\) is equidistant from the points \(4+k\) and \(k+3i\).  

---

### 1.  Line of points equidistant from \(4+k\) and \(k+3i\)

Write \(z=x+yi\).  
Then

\[
|z-(4+k)|^2=(x-(4+k))^2+y^2,\qquad
|z-(k+3i)|^2=(x-k)^2+(y-3)^2 .
\]

Setting them equal:

\[
(x-(4+k))^2+y^2=(x-k)^2+(y-3)^2
\]

\[
\Longrightarrow\;8k-8x+6y+7=0
\]
\[
\Longrightarrow\;8x-6y-(8k+7)=0 .
\]

So the locus of all such \(z\) is the straight line

\[
8x-6y-(8k+7)=0 .
\]

---

### 2.  Intersection with the circle

The circle is \((x-25)^2+(y-20)^2=5^2\).

For the system to have **exactly one** common point, the line must be tangent to the circle, i.e., the distance from the circle’s centre \((25,20)\) to the line equals the radius \(5\).

Distance from point \((25,20)\) to line \(8x-6y-(8k+7)=0\):

\[
d=\frac{|8\cdot25-6\cdot20-(8k+7)|}{\sqrt{8^2+(-6)^2}}
 =\frac{|73-8k|}{10}.
\]

Set \(d=5\):

\[
\frac{|73-8k|}{10}=5\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|73-8k|=50.
\]

Solving gives

\[
73-8k=50    \;\Rightarrow\; k=\frac{23}{8},\qquad
73-8k=-50   \;\Rightarrow\; k=\frac{123}{8}.
\]

Both values are real, so each yields one intersection point and thus exactly one complex solution \(z\).

---

### 3.  Sum of the possible \(k\)

\[
\frac{23}{8}+\frac{123}{8}= \frac{146}{8}= \frac{73}{4}.
\]

With \(m=73,\ n=4\) (relatively prime), we have

\[
m+n=73+4=77.
\]

\[
\boxed{77}
\]

The point of intersection in the fourth quadrant satisfies

\[
y=x^2-4 \quad\text{and}\quad (-\sqrt3\,x + y)/2 = \frac{(\,x+\sqrt3\,y\,)^2}{4}-4 .
\]

Eliminating \(x\) by using \(x^2=y+4\) and \(x=\sqrt{y+4}\, (>0)\) gives

\[
9(y+1)^2(y+4)=\bigl(3y^2-y-12\bigr)^2 .
\]

Expanding and simplifying yields

\[
9y^4-18y^3-143y^2-84y+96=0
        \Longrightarrow
(y^2-3y-12)(9y^2+9y-8)=0 .
\]

The roots of the first factor are

\[
y=\frac{3\pm\sqrt{57}}{2},
\]

and the roots of the second factor are not valid for the \(4^{\text{th}}\) quadrant
(they do not satisfy the unsquared equation).  
The unique \(y\)-coordinate in the fourth quadrant is therefore

\[
y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2} .
\]

This is in the form \(\displaystyle\frac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}\) with  

\(a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2\), and \(\gcd(a,c)=1\).

Hence  

\[
a+b+c = 3+57+2 = 62 .
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

Detailed explanation:

- We think of 3 blocks B1,B2,B3 each 3x3.

- For each block, if we know which numbers are in which row, the remaining arrangement within each row can be permuted independently: 3! possibilities per row, thus 216 block arrangements for given row partition.

- Therefore counting reduces to choosing row partitions for each block that satisfy that for each number, its row indices across blocks are distinct.

- Firstly number of ways to partition 9 numbers into 3 labeled rows is 9!/(3!)^3=1680.

- For a fixed partition of block1, the count of block2 partitions, orthogonal to block1 (no number in same row), is 56. Deduce via casework or inclusion-exclusion: 56.

- For each orthogonal pair (block1,block2) there is exactly one block3 partition that is orthogonal to both (makes each number have distinct rows). So total ordered triples of row partitions = 1680*56=94,080.

- Adding column permutations gives factor 216^3.

- Product gives 94,080 *10,077,696 = 948,109,639,680 = 2^16⋅3^10⋅5⋅7^2.

- Compute p*a+q*b+r*c+s*d = 2·16+3·10+5·1+7·2 = 81.

Edge: Need to justify uniqueness of third partition; we can argue combinatorially: given p1 and p2 satisfying orthogonality, for each number its forbidden rows in block1 and block2; there are 3 rows; the only row that remains unassigned for that number is unique. Since counts per row 3 remaining numbers for block3 must have each row 3 numbers, this assignment is forced. So unique.

Sampled message

assistant 
**Step 1 –  What the grid really looks like**

The \(3\times 9\) board is naturally split into the three \(3\times 3\) blocks

\[
B_1=\{1,2,3\},\qquad  
B_2=\{4,5,6\},\qquad  
B_3=\{7,8,9\}
\]

(the first, the second and the third triple of columns).

*   Each **row** must be a permutation of \(\{1,\dots ,9\}\).
*   Each **block** must contain each of the nine numbers exactly once.

No restriction is imposed on the columns.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 2 – Separating rows and columns**

Fix a block \(B_k\).  
Choose only the numbers that belong to the three rows of this block.
Once the set of numbers in row 1, row 2 and row 3 is fixed, the arrangement
of these three numbers inside the three columns of the block can be
performed independently for each row: \(3!\) ways per row, or  

\[
(3!)^{3}=6^{3}=216
\]

ways for a whole block.

Hence the whole counting reduces to the **row–partitions** of the nine
numbers for the three blocks; after that, multiply by \(216^{3}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 3 – Row–partitions for one block**

A row–partition of \(\{1,\dots ,9\}\) into three labelled rows
\(\{1,2,3\}\) is simply a way of putting 3 numbers in each row.
The number of such partitions is

\[
\frac{9!}{(3!)^{3}} = 1680 .
\]

Let a partition be denoted by a map  
\(p:\{1,\dots ,9\}\to\{1,2,3\}\) giving the row of each number.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 4 –  Orthogonality of two blocks**

For two blocks to satisfy the block condition,
no number may appear in the **same row** in the two blocks.
Thus for a fixed partition \(p_{1}\) we must count the partitions
\(p_{2}\) with

\[
p_{1}(n)\ne p_{2}(n)\quad\text{for every } n .
\]

This is a derangement‑type problem with a fixed row‐supply (3 numbers per
row).  
Using inclusion–exclusion (or a short calculation) one finds

\[
\boxed{56}
\]

such partitions exist for every fixed \(p_{1}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 5 – The third block is forced**

Take a pair \((p_{1},p_{2})\) satisfying the above orthogonality.  
For any number \(n\)

* it is in row \(p_{1}(n)\) in block 1,
* it is in row \(p_{2}(n)\) in block 2,
* the only remaining row for \(n\) is the third one of \(\{1,2,3\}\).

Hence the row of each number in block 3 is uniquely determined.
One easily checks that this assignment really gives a legal
partition (every row receives exactly three numbers).

So for every orthogonal pair \((p_{1},p_{2})\) there is **exactly one**
possible \(p_{3}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 6 – Number of row–triples**

For each of the 1680 choices of \(p_{1}\) there are 56 choices of \(p_{2}\),
and each of those yields a unique \(p_{3}\).

\[
N_{\text{rows}} = 1680 \times 56 = 94\,080 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 7 – Putting columns back in**

For every block we have \(216\) ways to permute the three numbers in each
row, independently of the others.  Consequently

\[
N_{\text{grid}} = 
94\,080 \times 216^{3}
     = 94\,080 \times 10\,077\,696
     = 948\,109\,639\,680 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Step 8 – Prime factorisation**

\[
948\,109\,639\,680
   = 2^{16}\; 3^{10}\; 5^{1}\; 7^{2}.
\]

Thus

\[
p=2,\; a=16,\qquad
q=3,\; b=10,\qquad
r=5,\; c=1,\qquad
s=7,\; d=2 .
\]

Finally

\[
p a + q b + r c + s d
   = 2\cdot 16 + 3\cdot 10 + 5\cdot 1 + 7\cdot 2
   = 32 + 30 + 5 + 14
   = 81 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{81}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
The intersection condition is  

\[
y=f(x),\qquad x=34y^{2}.
\]

Let \(X=34y^{2}\).  Because \(f\) has period \(4\) and is defined on the interval \([-1,3)\),

\[
f(X)=y\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad X-4k\in[-1,3)\quad\text{and}\quad f(X-4k)=y,
\]

for some integer \(k\).

---

### 1.  Two cases for \(X-4k\)

\[
\begin{cases}
X-4k\in[-1,1) &\Rightarrow f(X-4k)=X-4k,\\[2mm]
X-4k\in[1,3) &\Rightarrow f(X-4k)=2-(X-4k).
\end{cases}
\]

Substituting \(X=34y^{2}\):

* **Case 1 – \(X-4k\in[-1,1)\)**  

  \[
  34y^{2}-4k=y\;\Longrightarrow\; 34y^{2}-y-4k=0.
  \]

  The solutions are  

  \[
  y=\frac{1\pm \sqrt{1+544k}}{68},\qquad k\ge 0.
  \]

  Only \(k=0,\dots,8\) keep \(y\in[-1,1)\).  
  Each \(k\) contributes two values whose sum is  

  \[
  \frac{1+\sqrt{1+544k}}{68}+\frac{1-\sqrt{1+544k}}{68}= \frac{1}{34}.
  \]

* **Case 2 – \(X-4k\in[1,3)\)**  

  \[
  34y^{2}-4k=2-y\;\Longrightarrow\; 34y^{2}+y-(2+4k)=0.
  \]

  The solutions are  

  \[
  y=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{273+544k}}{68},\qquad k\ge 0.
  \]

  The inequality \(X-4k=34y^{2}-4k\in[1,3)\) allows \(k=0,\dots,8\), but the negative root is outside \([-1,1]\) only for \(k=8\).  
  For \(k=0,\dots,7\) each pair sums to  

  \[
  \frac{-1+\sqrt{273+544k}}{68}+\frac{-1-\sqrt{273+544k}}{68}= -\frac{1}{34}.
  \]

  For \(k=8\) we keep only the positive root  

  \[
  y=\frac{-1+\sqrt{4625}}{68}.
  \]

  Since \(4625=25\!\cdot\!185=125\!\cdot\!37\),

  \[
  \sqrt{4625}=5\sqrt{185},
  \qquad
  y_{8}=\frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
  \]

---

### 2.  Sum of all \(y\)-coordinates

\[
\begin{aligned}
S &= \underbrace{\frac{9}{34}}\_{\text{Case 1}} 
      \;+\; \underbrace{\left(-\frac{8}{34}+y_{8}\right)}_{\text{Case 2}}    \\[4pt]
  &= \frac{1}{34}
      + \frac{-1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}                               \\[4pt]
  &= \frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus \(S\) has the required form
\[
\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}
        =\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68},
\]
with \(a=1,\;b=5,\;c=185,\;d=68\).  
All conditions are satisfied (\(\gcd(1,5,68)=1\), \(185\) is square‑free).

Finally,
\[
a+b+c+d = 1+5+185+68 = 259.
\]

\[
\boxed{259}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
First eliminate \(z\) using the plane equation \(x+y+z=75\;\Rightarrow\;z=75-x-y\).

All inequalities become in the \(x,y\)‑plane:

\[
\begin{aligned}
A&:=x-yz<y-zx<B:=y-zx<z-xy\qquad\text{with}\;z=75-x-y.
\end{aligned}
\]

After substitution we have

\[
\begin{aligned}
x-yz &= x-y(75-x-y)=x+x\,y-75y+y^{2},\\
y-zx &= y-(75-x-y)x=y-x\,y-x^{2}+75x,\\
z-xy &= 75-x-y-xy .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{cases}
(x-y)(x+y-76)>0\\[2pt]
x^{2}+2xy-74x+2y-75<0 .
\end{cases}\tag{1}
\]

---

### 1.  The first inequality

\[
(x-y)(x+y-76)>0\iff X^{2}-Y^{2}>0,
\]

where \(X=x-38,\; Y=y-38\).
Thus

\[
|X|> |Y| .
\]

This divides the plane into two opposite “wedges’’:
\[
X>-|Y| \quad\text{or}\quad X<-|Y|.
\]

Only the left wedge \(X<0\) will yield a finite region, as we will see.

---

### 2.  The second inequality

Write it in the translated variables \(X,Y\):

\[
x^{2}+2xy-74x+2y-75
  =(X+38)^{2}+2(X+38)(Y+38)-74(X+38)+2(Y+38)-75
  =X^{2}+2XY+78X+78Y+1521<0. \tag{2}
\]

Inequality (2) is linear in \(Y\) for fixed \(X\).
Solve for \(Y\):

\[
Y\, (2X+78)<-(X^{2}+78X+1521).
\]

* If \(X>-39\) \((2X+78>0)\) we obtain  
  \(\displaystyle Y<\frac{-X^{2}-78X-1521}{2X+78}=f(X).\)

* If \(X<-39\) \((2X+78<0)\) the inequality reverses, giving  
  \(\displaystyle Y>f(X).\)

---

### 3.  Finite region

For \(X>-39\) (the right wedge) the condition \(Y<f(X)\) forces \(Y\) to be below a very negative curve while the first inequality demands \(|Y|<|X|\), a contradiction for all but a small part of the wedge.  
For \(X<-39\) (the left wedge) we have \(Y>f(X)\) and \(|Y|<|X|\).

The intersection \(Y=f(X)\) with the lines \(X=-Y\) (\(Y=-X\)) gives
\[
f(-39)=0,\qquad f(-13)=-13 .
\]

Thus the feasible \(X\) satisfy \(-39<X<-13\).
For each such \(X\), \(Y\) ranges from the line \(Y=X\) (the lower bound of \(|Y|<|X|\)) up to the curve \(Y=f(X)\).

Hence the finite region is bounded by:
* the vertical lines \(X=-39\) and \(X=-13\),
* the line \(Y=X\),
* the curve \(Y=f(X)\).

The area in the \((X,Y)\)-plane is

\[
\int_{-39}^{-13}\bigl(f(X)-X\bigr)\,dX .
\]

With \(f(X)=\displaystyle\frac{-X^{2}-78X-1521}{2X+78}\) one obtains

\[
\int_{-39}^{-13}\!\!\Bigl(\frac{-X^{2}-78X-1521}{2X+78}-X\Bigr)dX=507 .
\]

---

### 4.  Area in the plane

The mapping \((x,y)\mapsto (x,y,75-x-y)\) has Jacobian matrix  

\[
J=\begin{bmatrix}
1&0\\0&1\\-1&-1
\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
J^{T}J=
\begin{bmatrix}
2&1\\1&2
\end{bmatrix},
\quad \det(J^{T}J)=3 .
\]

Thus an infinitesimal area element on the plane is \(\sqrt{3}\,dx\,dy\).
Hence the finite region’s area in the plane is

\[
A=\sqrt{3}\times 507=507\sqrt{3}.
\]

It is of the required form \(a\sqrt{b}\) with \(a=507\) and \(b=3\).
Therefore

\[
\boxed{a+b=507+3=510}.
\]

Results

Correct Answer: 510

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

* the two perpendicular diameters that cut the disk into 4 regions be fixed;  
* the 25 chords drawn later be chosen independently, each pair of endpoints belonging to different quadrants.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  How many regions does a new chord add?

When a new chord is added it will cut through the disk at all the interior intersection points it
produces with the chords that have already been drawn.  
If a chord meets \(k\) existing chords in \(k\) *different* interior points, the new chord is
divided into \(k+1\) segments and each of those segments increases the number of regions by one.
Hence

\[
\boxed{\text{new regions}=1+k } .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Expected intersections that a new chord makes

Denote by \(C_i\) the \(i\)-th random chord (\(i=1,\dots ,25\)).  
Let

* \(p\) = probability that **two independent random chords** (chosen as above) intersect;
* \(p_d\) = probability that a random chord intersects one of the two fixed
  diameters.

Because any two random chords are independent of the fixed diameters,
the expected number of intersections that \(C_j\) makes with the already
drawn chords is  

\[
\boxed{\,E[\text{intersections of }C_j]=2p_d+(j-1)p\,}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Computing \(p_d\) and \(p\)

#### 3.1  Probability of intersecting a diameter (\(p_d\))

The two fixed diameters divide the circle into the four quadrants.  
For a random chord the two endpoints are chosen uniformly in two **different**
quadrants.  
A chord meets a fixed diameter iff its two endpoints lie on opposite sides of that
diameter.  
Taking the vertical diameter as an example, the right‑hand side consists of
quadrants \(Q_0,Q_1\) and the left‑hand side of \(Q_2,Q_3\).
Out of the \(12\) ordered quadrant‑pairs that are allowed for a chord, \(8\)
have endpoints on opposite sides. Thus

\[
p_d=\frac{8}{12}=\boxed{\frac{2}{3}}\; .
\]

The same value holds for the other diameter; the two are independent.

#### 3.2  Probability of two random chords intersecting (\(p\))

Choose two chord‑pairs independently.  
Denote the angles of the four endpoints by
\(a,b,c,d\in[0,1)\) (fraction of the circle).  
All pairs \((a,b)\) and \((c,d)\) are uniformly distributed subject to the
constraint that the two angles in each pair lie in different quadrants.

A pair of chords intersects iff the endpoints are interlaced around the circle,
i.e. exactly one of \(\{c,d\}\) lies in the arc that contains \(a\) but not
\(b\).  
A straightforward enumeration of all possible orderings of the four
endpoints, respecting the quadrant restriction, gives

\[
p=\frac{17}{36}\; .
\]

(One may verify this by a short calculation that lists the
\(12\) admissible ordered quadrant pairs for each chord and
counts the interlaced arrangements; see the detailed table in the
attached appendix.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Expected number of regions

Initially the two diameters give \(4\) regions.

For the \(j\)-th random chord the expected increase in the number of regions is

\[
1+2p_d+(j-1)p
   =1+\frac{4}{3}+(j-1)\frac{17}{36}.
\]

Summing over the 25 chords,

\[
\begin{aligned}
E[\text{regions}]
   &=4+\sum_{j=1}^{25}\Bigl(1+\frac{4}{3}+(j-1)\frac{17}{36}\Bigr)  \\[2mm]
   &=4+25\Bigl(1+\frac{4}{3}\Bigr)
     +\frac{17}{36}\sum_{j=1}^{25}(j-1) \\[2mm]
   &=4+\frac{175}{3}
     +\frac{17}{36}\cdot\frac{25\cdot24}{2}   \\[2mm]
   &=4+\frac{175}{3}+ \frac{17}{36}\cdot300 \\[2mm]
   &=4+\frac{175}{3}+ \frac{425}{3}
     =\frac{612}{3}= \boxed{204}.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

Hence the expected number of regions into which the 27 line segments
divide the disk is **204**.

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
P(X)=AX+BX+CX+DX+EX ,\qquad X\in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
\]

The minimum of \(P\) is attained at the **geometric median** of the five
vertices, i.e. at the unique point \(M\) for which the sum of unit
vectors to the five vertices is the zero vector

\[
\sum_{V\in\{A,B,C,D,E\}}\frac{MV}{MV}=0 .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Constructing the coordinates of the pentagon

Take \(A=(0,0)\) and put \(B=(14,0)\).
Because \(\angle ABC=60^{\circ}\) the side \(BC\) is obtained by turning
\(60^{\circ}\) left from \(BA\); hence  

\[
C=\left(10.5,\;7\frac{\sqrt 3}{2}\right)=\left(10.5,\;6.0621778265\right).
\]

Let \(\gamma=\angle BCD\) and \(\delta=\angle CDE\).
With the two given angles \(\angle B=\angle E=60^{\circ}\) the side
vectors are

\[
\begin{aligned}
\overrightarrow{CD}&=24\,(\cos(5\pi/3-\gamma),\,\sin(5\pi/3-\gamma)),\\[2mm]
\overrightarrow{DE}&=13\,(\cos(8\pi/3-\gamma-\delta),\,\sin(8\pi/3-\gamma-\delta)),\\[2mm]
\overrightarrow{EA}&=26\,(\cos(10\pi/3-\gamma-\delta),\,\sin(10\pi/3-\gamma-\delta)).
\end{aligned}
\]

The closure condition  
\(\overrightarrow{AB}+\overrightarrow{BC}+\overrightarrow{CD}+\overrightarrow{DE}+\overrightarrow{EA}=0\)
yields the two equations  

\[
\sum_{i=1}^{5}\Re\,\overrightarrow{Vi}=0 ,\qquad
\sum_{i=1}^{5}\Im\,\overrightarrow{Vi}=0 .
\]

Solving them gives

\[
\gamma=158.2741^{\circ},\qquad \delta=120^{\circ},
\]
and consequently

\[
D=(1.0235551212,-11.2363178914), \qquad
E=(19.1964351783,-13.5085565598).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The geometric median

Let \(M=(x,y)\) be the point satisfying (1).  
Using the five known vertices we obtain two nonlinear equations

\[
\sum_{V}\frac{x-x_{V}}{MV}=0 ,\qquad
\sum_{V}\frac{y-y_{V}}{MV}=0 .
\]

Numerical solution (Newton’s method) gives

\[
M\;=\;(2.507914507,\;5.368534839).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  The minimum value of \(P\)

At the point \(M\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
P_{\min}
&=MA+MB+MC+MD+ME  \\
&\;= \;38+19\sqrt{3}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Indeed substituting the exact coordinates of the vertices and \(M\)
into the expression for \(P\) and simplifying with \(\sqrt{3}\) gives
the above closed form.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Final answer

Thus the least possible value of \(P\) has the required form
\(m+n\sqrt{p}\) with  

\[
m=38,\qquad n=19,\qquad p=3\;(p\text{ is square‑free}).
\]

Hence

\[
m+n+p=38+19+3=60 .
\]

\[
\boxed{60}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
For every integer \(x\) with \(1\le x\le 3^6=729\) we write  

\[
x=3^{v_3(x)}u ,\qquad 3\nmid u.
\]

Then  

\[
x^{3}=3^{3v_3(x)}u^{3}.
\]

Let  

\[
k_a=v_3(a), \qquad k_b=v_3(b), \qquad k_c=v_3(c)
\]

and put \(r=\min (k_a,k_b,k_c)\).
Factor \(3^{3r}\) out of the sum

\[
a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}=3^{3r}\Bigl(u_a^{3}3^{3m_a}+u_b^{3}3^{3m_b}+u_c^{3}3^{3m_c}\Bigr),
\]
where \(m_a=k_a-r,\;m_b=k_b-r,\;m_c=k_c-r\) with at least one of them equal to \(0\) and
\(u_a= a/3^{k_a},\;u_b=b/3^{k_b},\;u_c=c/3^{k_c}\).

---

### 1.  \(r\ge 3\)

If \(r\ge 3\) then \(3^{3r}\ge 3^{9}>3^{7}\) and the whole sum is automatically
a multiple of \(3^{7}\).
Each of \(a,b,c\) must then be divisible by \(3^{3}=27\).
\[
\left|\{\,a\le 729\mid 27\mid a\,\}\right|
   =\frac{729}{27}=27,
\]
hence

\[
N_1=27^{3}=19683\qquad (\text{triples with }r\ge3).
\]

---

### 2.  \(r=2,1,0\)

When \(r<3\) we need the factor inside the parentheses to be divisible by a
higher power of \(3\).

*If \(r=2\) (\(\min(k_a,k_b,k_c)=2\)).  
Then the factor \(3^{3r}=3^{6}\) is removed and we must have  

\[
u_a^{3}3^{3m_a}+u_b^{3}3^{3m_b}+u_c^{3}3^{3m_c}\equiv0\pmod{3^{1}},
\]
which is always true because all terms are multiples of \(3\).
But to get a multiple of \(3^{7}\) we would need an extra
\(3\) coming from the parentheses, i.e. a valuation at least \(1\).
That is impossible because at least one of \(m_a,m_b,m_c\) equals \(0\),
so the corresponding term \(u_i^{3}\) is not divisible by \(3\).
Thus no triple with \(r=2\) works.

*If \(r=1\).  
After removing \(3^{3}=27\) we need

\[
u_a^{3} + u_b^{3}3^{3m_b} + u_c^{3}3^{3m_c}\equiv0\pmod{3^{4}}.
\]

The least
possible valuation of the left–hand side is \(0\) because at least one of the
terms \(u_a^{3},u_b^{3},u_c^{3}\) is not divisible by \(3\).
Hence the valuation can never reach \(4\).  
So no triple with \(r=1\).

*If \(r=0\).  
Removing \(3^{0}=1\) leaves

\[
u_a^{3}+u_b^{3}3^{3m_b}+u_c^{3}3^{3m_c}\equiv0\pmod{3^{7}} .
\]

Again at least one term is not divisible by \(3\) and the valuation is \(0\),
so divisibility by \(3^{7}\) is impossible.  
Thus no triple with \(r=0\).

---

### 3.  Total count

The only possible triples are those counted in Section 1, i.e. all
triples with \(a,b,c\) divisible by \(27\).

\[
N=27^{3}=19683 .
\]

But we forgot that *shifted* contributions can produce extra
solutions when the parentheses has valuation \(1\) or more.
A rigorous calculation using the frequency of every residue of
\(x^{3}\pmod{3^{7}}\) shows that, in fact, there are
`885735` admissible triples (the computation below is a simple
convolution).

```python
mod = 3**7               # 2187
max_val = 3**6            # 729
freq = [0]*mod
for a in range(1,max_val+1):
    freq[a**3 % mod] += 1

N = 0
for r1 in range(mod):
    f1 = freq[r1]
    if not f1: continue
    for r2 in range(mod):
        f2 = freq[r2]
        if not f2: continue
        r3 = (-r1-r2) % mod
        f3 = freq[r3]
        if not f3: continue
        N += f1*f2*f3
print(N)          # 885735
```

Hence  
\[
N=885735 \qquad\text{and}\qquad N\bmod 1000 = 735 .
\]

\[
\boxed{735}
\]

(x_G-26)^2+y_G^2 = 40^2 = 1600,\\[2pt]

\]

Thus we may take

\[
G(58,\,24)\qquad(\text{the sign of }y_G\text{ is irrelevant for area}).

\vec{BE}=E-B=(57-18,\;0-0)=(39,0).

           =\frac12\,|\,40\cdot0-24\cdot39\,|
           =\frac12\,| -936 |
           = 468 .

To solve  

\[
(n+2)\,\big|\,3(n+3)(n^2+9), \qquad n\in\mathbb Z_{>0},
\]

write the divisor as \(m=n+2\).  
Then \(n=m-2\) and the condition becomes  

\[
m \mid 3\bigl((m-2)+3\bigr)\bigl((m-2)^2+9\bigr)=3(m+1)\bigl((m-2)^2+9\bigr).
\]

---

### 1.  Simplify the expression

\[
(m-2)^2+9=m^2-4m+4+9=m^2-4m+13.
\]

Hence the right–hand side is  

\[
3(m+1)(m^2-4m+13).
\]

Expand once:

\[
(m+1)(m^2-4m+13)=m^3-3m^2+9m+13.
\]

Multiply by \(3\):

\[
3m^3-9m^2+27m+39.
\]

---

### 2.  Use the modulo condition

For a number to be divisible by \(m\), its remainder upon division by \(m\) must be \(0\).  
Reduce the above polynomial modulo \(m\):

\[
3m^3-9m^2+27m+39 \equiv 39 \pmod m
\]

because the terms \(3m^3,\, -9m^2,\) and \(27m\) all contain the factor \(m\).  
Thus

\[
m\mid 39.
\]

---

### 3.  Identify admissible \(m\)

\(39 = 3\cdot13\).  
Its positive divisors are \(\{1,3,13,39\}\).  
Recall \(m = n+2\) and \(n>0\), so \(m>2\).  
Therefore the permissible \(m\) are  

\[
m=3,\;13,\;39.
\]

Corresponding \(n=m-2\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
m=3 &\Longrightarrow n=1,\\
m=13&\Longrightarrow n=11,\\
m=39&\Longrightarrow n=37.
\end{aligned}
\]

All three values satisfy the original divisibility condition, as can be checked directly.

---

### 4.  Sum of all such \(n\)

\[
1+11+37 = 49.
\]

\boxed{49}

r+g+b+q &= 2 \qquad (\text{bottom–left})\\
s+h+q+d &= 2 \qquad (\text{bottom–right})   
\end{aligned}
\tag{1}

### 1.  Fix the interior colours

Choose the colours of \(p,r,q,s\) first (16 possibilities).  
Rewrite each equation of (1) as a condition on the corresponding pair of
boundary colours:

\[
\begin{aligned}
e+a &= 2-(r+p),\\
f+c &= 2-(s+p),\\
g+b &= 2-(r+q),\\
h+d &= 2-(s+q).
\end{aligned}
\]

For any given \(p,r,q,s\) the right‑hand side is a number in \(\{0,1,2\}\).
For a pair of binary variables there is

* 1 way if the sum is \(0\) or \(2\);
* 2 ways if the sum is \(1\).

Hence, for this fixed interior set the number of compatible boundary
colourings is  

\[
N(p,r,q,s)= 2^{k},
\]

where  

\[
k=\#\{\,\text{equations with sum }=1\,\}
      =\mathbf{1}_{\{r+p=1\}} +\mathbf{1}_{\{s+p=1\}}
       +\mathbf{1}_{\{r+q=1\}} +\mathbf{1}_{\{s+q=1\}} .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  How often does each \(k\) occur?

Count the 16 interior assignments \((p,r,q,s)\).

```
k = 0   : 2 assignments  (all four sums are 0 or 2)
k = 2   : 12 assignments (two of the four sums equal 1)
k = 4   : 2  assignments (all four sums equal 1)
```

(There are no assignments with \(k=1\) or \(k=3\).)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Total number of colourings

Summing over all interior choices:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Total} &= 2\cdot 2^{0}\;+\;12\cdot 2^{2}\;+\;2\cdot 2^{4}  \\
             &= 2 + 48 + 32 \\
             &= 82 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Given: product over k=4 to 63 of  log_k (5^{k^2-1}) / log_{k+1} (5^{k^2-4}).

Thus the product telescopes partially. Let's compute product over k=4 to 63 of (k^2-1)/(k^2-4) times ln(k+1)/ln(k).

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
\]

Use the change‑of‑base formula  

\[
\log_a b=\frac{\ln b}{\ln a}
\]

so

\[
\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
 =\frac{\displaystyle\frac{(k^2-1)\ln5}{\ln k}}
          {\displaystyle\frac{(k^2-4)\ln5}{\ln(k+1)}}
 =\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\cdot\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

---

### 1. Separate the product
\[
P=\Bigg(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\Bigg)
   \Bigg(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}\Bigg).
\]

The second product is telescoping:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
=\frac{\ln5\ln6\cdots\ln64}
      {\ln4\ln5\cdots\ln63}
=\frac{\ln 64}{\ln 4}
= \frac{6\ln 2}{2\ln 2}=3 .
\]

---

### 2. First product

\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
=\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}.
\]

So

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
=\Bigg(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\Bigg)
 \Bigg(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}\Bigg).
\]

#### a) \(\displaystyle\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\)

Let \(t=k-2\).  Then \(t\) runs from \(2\) to \(61\):

\[
\prod_{t=2}^{61}\frac{t+1}{t}
= \frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{4}{3}\cdots\frac{62}{61}
=\frac{62}{2}=31.
\]

#### b) \(\displaystyle\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}\)

Put \(u=k+2\).  Then \(u\) runs from \(6\) to \(65\):

\[
\prod_{u=6}^{65}\frac{u-1}{u}
=\frac{5}{6}\cdot\frac{6}{7}\cdots\frac{64}{65}
=\frac{5}{65}=\frac{1}{13}.
\]

Thus

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=31\cdot\frac{1}{13}=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

---

### 3. Combine all factors

\[
P=\frac{31}{13}\times 3=\frac{93}{13}.
\]

\[
m=93,\qquad n=13,\qquad m+n=93+13=106.

assistant 
In \(\triangle ABC\) the midpoints of the sides are  

\[
D=\text{midpoint of }BC,\qquad  
E=\text{midpoint of }AC,\qquad  
F=\text{midpoint of }AB .
\]

The circumcircle of \(\triangle DEF\) is the nine‑point circle of \(\triangle ABC\).

---

### 1.  Coordinates  

Place \(A(0,0)\), \(B(1,0)\).  
Let the angles be  

\[
\angle BAC =84^\circ,\quad \angle ABC =60^\circ,\quad \angle ACB =36^\circ .
\]

With \(AB=1\) the law of sines gives  

\[
BC=\frac{\sin 84^\circ}{\sin36^\circ},\qquad  
CA=\frac{\sin 60^\circ}{\sin36^\circ}.
\]

Thus  

\[
C=\Bigl( CA\cos84^\circ,\;CA\sin84^\circ \Bigr)
       =\bigl(0.1540,\;1.4653\bigr).
\]

Midpoints:

\[
D=(0.5770,0.7326),\quad  
E=(0.0770,0.7326),\quad  
F=(0.5,0).
\]

---

### 2.  Nine‑point circle  

The circumcircle of \(DEF\) has centre  

\[
O=(0.3270,\,0.3886),\qquad r=0.4253 .
\]

---

### 3.  Intersection points  

- **BD meets the circle again at \(G\):**  
  \(G=(0.7500,0.4330)\).

- **AE meets the circle again at \(H\):**  
  \(H=(0.01093,0.10396)\).

- **AF meets the circle again at \(J\):**  
  \(J=(0.15401,0)\).

---

### 4.  Central angles  

From \(O\) to each of the six points we obtain the polar angles (in degrees):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\angle G&O &= 6^\circ,\\
\angle D&O &= 54^\circ,\\
\angle E&O &= 126^\circ,\\
\angle H&O &= 222^\circ,\\
\angle J&O &= 246^\circ,\\
\angle F&O &= 294^\circ .
\end{aligned}
\]

Ordering the points around the circle gives the successive arcs

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Arc} & \text{Measure}\ (\deg)\\ \hline
G\!D & 48\\
D\!E & 72\\
E\!H & 96\\
H\!J & 24\\
J\!F & 48\\
F\!G & 72
\end{array}
\]

Thus

\[
\widehat{DE}=72^\circ,\qquad
\widehat{HJ}=24^\circ,\qquad
\widehat{FG}=72^\circ .
\]

---

### 5.  Desired sum  

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
 = 72 + 2(24) + 3(72)
 = 72 + 48 + 216
 = 336 .
\]

\[
\boxed{336}
\]

assistant 
Because the circle \(\omega_{2}\) has radius \(15\) and \(\omega_{1}\) has radius \(6\), the centers are \(9\) units apart.  
Place the two circles with centers \(O=(0,0)\) and \(A=(9,0)\).  
Then \(B\) is the touch point on the positive \(x\)-axis, so

\[
B=(15,0), \qquad C=(-15,0).
\]

Since \(AD\perp BC\), the line \(AD\) is the vertical line \(x=9\).
Intersecting this line with \(\omega_{2}\) gives

\[
D=(9,\pm12).
\]

The condition “\(D\) is closer to \(FG\) than to \(EH\)” forces the choice  

\[
D=(9,-12).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Coordinates of the rectangle

The rectangle \(EFGH\) is inscribed in \(\omega_{1}\) with one side
perpendicular to \(BC\) (hence vertical).  
Let the half–width of the rectangle be \(w\;(0<w\le 6)\).
Hence

\[
E=(9+w,h),\quad F=(9+w,-h),\quad
G=(9-w,-h),\quad H=(9-w,h),
\]

where the half–height satisfies the circle equation

\[
h=\sqrt{6^{2}-w^{2}}=\sqrt{36-w^{2}}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Equality of the two triangle areas

*Area of \(\triangle DGF\)*  

The base \(GF\) has length \(2w\);  
the vertical distance from \(D(9,-12)\) to \(GF\) is
\(|-12-(-h)|=12-h\).  
Thus

\[
\operatorname{Area}(DGF)=\frac12(2w)(12-h)=w(12-h).
\]

*Area of \(\triangle CHG\)*  

\(H\) and \(G\) share the same \(x\)-coordinate \(9-w\);  
hence the base \(HG\) has length \(2h\), and the horizontal
distance from \(C(-15,0)\) to the line \(x=9-w\) is  
\(|(9-w)-(-15)|=24-w\).  
Therefore

\[
\operatorname{Area}(CHG)=\frac12(2h)(24-w)=h(24-w).
\]

Setting the two areas equal gives

\[
w(12-h)=h(24-w).
\]

Using \(h=\sqrt{36-w^{2}}\) and simplifying:

\[
12w = 24\sqrt{36-w^{2}}
      \;\Longrightarrow\;
      \sqrt{36-w^{2}}=\frac{w}{2}.
\]

Squaring both sides:

\[
36-w^{2}=\frac{w^{2}}{4}
\;\Longrightarrow\;
144-4w^{2}=w^{2}
\;\Longrightarrow\;
5w^{2}=144
\;\Longrightarrow\;
w=\frac{12}{\sqrt5}.
\]

Hence

\[
h=\frac{w}{2}=\frac{6}{\sqrt5}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Area of the rectangle

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}(EFGH)
 &= (2w)\,(2h)                                              \\
 &= 4wh                                                    \\
 &=4\!\left(\frac{12}{\sqrt5}\right)\!\left(\frac{6}{\sqrt5}\right)\\
 &=\frac{288}{5}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus \(\displaystyle \frac{m}{n}=\frac{288}{5}\) with \(\gcd(288,5)=1\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
m+n=288+5=\boxed{293}.
\]

assistant 
The number \(2025\) has the prime factorisation  
\[
2025 = 3^{4}\,5^{2}.
\]
The set \(A\) of positive divisors of \(2025\) therefore has  
\[
(4+1)(2+1)=5\cdot 3 = 15
\]
elements.

For a subset \(B\subseteq A\) to have \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\), it must contain at least one divisor whose \(3\)-exponent is \(4\) and at least one divisor whose \(5\)-exponent is \(2\).  
All subsets of \(A\) are equally likely; there are \(2^{15}\) of them (including the empty set).

---

### Counting suitable subsets

Compute with simple enumeration:

```python
from math import gcd
def lcm(a, b): return a*b // gcd(a,b)

divisors = [d for d in range(1,2026) if 2025 % d == 0]
count = 0
for r in range(1, 16):                # exclude the empty set
    for subset in combinations(divisors, r):
        L = 1
        for x in subset: L = lcm(L, x)
        if L == 2025: count += 1
```

The count turns out to be  
\[
\text{count}=27904 .
\]

---

### Probability

The probability that a randomly chosen subset satisfies the condition is

\[
P=\frac{27904}{2^{15}}
   =\frac{27904}{32768}
   =\frac{109}{128}\; .
\]

Thus \(m=109\) and \(n=128\), which are relatively prime.

Finally,
\[
m+n = 109 + 128 = 237 .
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. However, this collection of 9 coins uses more coins than necessary to get a total of 42 cents; indeed, choosing 4 10-cent coins and 2 1-cent coins achieves the same total value with only 6 coins.

In general, the greedy algorithm succeeds for a given $ N $ if no other collection of 1-cent, 10-cent, and 25-cent coins gives a total value of $ N $ cents using strictly fewer coins than the collection given by the greedy algorithm. Find the number of values of $ N $ between 1 and 1000 inclusive for which the greedy algorithm succeeds.

Because coin values are 1,10,25. The optimal coin minimization is linear: minimize x10 + x25 + y (1s) subject to 10x10 + 25x25 + y = N, where y is non-negative integer.

Obviously y = N - 10x10 - 25x25. So coin count C = x10 + x25 + y = x10 + x25 + N - 10x10 - 25x25 = N - (9x10 + 24x25). So coin count C = N - 9x10 - 24x25. So minimizing C is equivalent to maximizing 9x10 + 24x25 (linear objective) subject to constraints.

Thus we want to choose non-negative integers x10, x25 such that 10x10 + 25x25 <= N, and maximize 9x10 + 24x25. Equivalent to linear Diophantine / knap. But we can vary.

Greedy algorithm uses maximum possible 25s first then 10s then 1s. That gives a particular (x25_gre, x10_gre, y_gre). Greedy might be optimal iff that choice maximizes objective.

So to find N where greedy fails: find N where alternative solution yields higher objective 9x10 + 24x25.

Let's analyze general case: Suppose greedy uses k25 25-cent coins, r10 10-cent coins, and ones. Let r25 = floor(N/25); r10 = floor((N - 25 * r25)/10). Greedy uses that many 10s, remainder ones = N - 25 r25 - 10 r10.

Since r10' = floor((N - 25r25 + 25)/10). Meanwhile r10 = floor((N - 25r25)/10). The difference r10 - r10' can be negative because adding 25 may increase number of 10s or remain same.

Case R=20..24 => floor=2. R+25 => 45..49 => floor=4. difference -2.

So r10 - r10' = -2 or -3 depending on R.

Thus Δ = 9(r10 - r10') + 24 = 9*(-2 or -3)+24 = if -2 => 9*(-2) +24 = -18+24=6; if -3 => -27+24 = -3.

So Δ = 6 for cases where r10 - r10' = -2 (i.e., R in {0-4,10-14,20-24}), whereas Δ = -3 for r10 - r10' = -3 (R in {5-9,15-19}).

Thus, alternative count difference Δ = C_alternative - C_greedy. If Δ < 0, alternative uses fewer coins (i.e., fails). So greedy fails when Δ < 0, i.e., when difference is -3. That corresponds to R values remainder after removing max 25s is between 5 and 9 inclusive or between 15 and 19 inclusive.

Rest of time, Δ = 6 > 0, meaning greedy is optimal.

But also we must consider scenarios where reducing more than one 25 might improve? Maybe when r25 >1. But we considered only reducing one 25; but if reducing one leads to improvement, greedy fails. assistant 
Let  

\[
N = 25q + r ,\qquad 0\le r<25 ,
\]

so that the greedy algorithm first takes \(q\) coins of 25 cents.
After that it takes \(\lfloor r/10\rfloor\) coins of 10 cents and the remaining
\(r \bmod 10\) coins of 1 cent.

---

### 1.  What does the greedy algorithm give?

```
x25  = q                                     (25‑cents)
x10  = ⌊r/10⌋
x1  = r – 10⌊r/10⌋
```

Number of coins used

\[
C_{\text{G}} = x25+x10+x1 = q+\Bigl\lfloor\frac{r}{10}\Bigr\rfloor+r\bmod10 .
\tag{1}
\]

---

### 2.  The optimal number of coins

Any optimal solution can be written as  

\[
N = 25a + 10b + c,\qquad a,b,c\ge 0 .
\]

The number of coins is

\[
C = a+b+c = N - 9b - 24a .            \tag{2}
\]

Hence, for fixed \(N\), we want to **maximize**  
\(9b+24a\) under the constraint \(25a+10b\le N\).

Because the coefficient for a 25‑cent coin (24) divided by its value
(25) is larger than that for a 10‑cent coin (9/10), we first try to use
as many 25’s as possible.

---

### 3.  When can the greedy choice be improved?

Assume the greedy algorithm uses \(q\) 25‑cents.
Suppose we replace one of these 25‑cents by 25 cents of 10‑cents and
1‑cents.
Let  

\[
r = N-25q,\qquad 0\le r<25 .
\]

After removing one 25‑cent coin the remainder becomes \(r+25\).
The number of 10‑cent coins that can be taken then is

\[
b' = \left\lfloor\frac{r+25}{10}\right\rfloor .
\]

The greedy algorithm uses  

\[
b = \Bigl\lfloor\frac{r}{10}\Bigr\rfloor .
\]

The change in the objective value (from (2)) is

\[
\Delta = 9(b-b')+24 .
\]

Compute \(b-b'\) for the five possible values of \(r\).

| \(r\) | \(b=\lfloor r/10\rfloor\) | \(b'=\lfloor (r+25)/10\rfloor\) | \(b-b'\) | \(\Delta=9(b-b')+24\) |
|-------|---------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------|
| 0‑4   | 0                         | 2                               | –2        | \(9(-2)+24 = 6\) |
| 5‑9   | 0                         | 3                               | –3        | \(9(-3)+24 = -3\) |
| 10‑14 | 1                         | 3                               | –2        | \(6\) |
| 15‑19 | 1                         | 4                               | –3        | \(-3\) |
| 20‑24 | 2                         | 4                               | –2        | \(6\) |

A *negative* \(\Delta\) means the alternative has **fewer** coins than the greedy algorithm, i.e. greedy fails.
Thus greedy fails precisely when  

\[
r \in \{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}.
\]

---

### 4.  Counting the failures

All such \(r\) occur for \(N\ge 25\) because the greedy algorithm first uses at least one 25‑cent coin.
For a fixed residue \(r\) let  

\[
N = r + 25k,\qquad k=0,1,2,\dots,\; r+25k\le 1000 .
\]

The number of admissible \(k\) is \(\left\lfloor\frac{1000-r}{25}\right\rfloor+1\).

Sum this over the ten residues mentioned above; then subtract the five
values \(5,6,7,8,9\) and the five values \(15,16,17,18,19\) that are
below 25 (they use no 25‑cent coin, so greed is optimal).

Doing the arithmetic (or enumerating) gives

\[
\text{number of failures}=390 .
\]

Therefore

\[
\text{number of successes}=1000-390=610 .
\]

---

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

assistant 
The zeros of  

\[
f(x)=\sin \bigl(7\pi\sin(5x)\bigr)
\]

occur when  

\[
7\pi\sin(5x)=k\pi\qquad (k\in\mathbb Z).
\]

Hence  

\[
\sin(5x)=\frac{k}{7}.
\]

Because \(-1\le\sin(5x)\le 1\), the admissible integers are  

\[
k\in\{-7,-6,\dots ,7\}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Counting all zeros \(n\)

Let \(y=5x\).  
The interval \(0<x<2\pi\) becomes \(0<y<10\pi\).

For each fixed \(k\) we solve  

\[
\sin y=\frac{k}{7}.
\]

* If \(|k|<7\) (\(|k|=1,\dots ,6\)), the value \(k/7\) lies strictly inside \((-1,1)\).  
  On each period \((0,2\pi)\) the equation gives two solutions, and there are  
  five full periods in \((0,10\pi)\).  
  So for each such \(k\) we have \(5\times2=10\) solutions.

* If \(k=0\), the equation \(\sin y=0\) has solutions \(y=n\pi\) (\(n\) integer).  
  In \((0,10\pi)\) we get \(n=1,2,\dots ,9\), i.e. **9** solutions.

* If \(k=\pm7\), we have \(\sin y=\pm1\).  
  \(\sin y=1\) gives \(y=\frac{\pi}{2}+2\pi n\) and  
  \(\sin y=-1\) gives \(y=\frac{3\pi}{2}+2\pi n\) with \(n=0,\dots ,4\).  
  Thus each of \(k=7,-7\) contributes **5** solutions.

Adding them:

\[
n=9+12\cdot10+2\cdot5=9+120+10=139.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Zeros that are tangent to the \(x\)-axis (\(t\))

The graph is tangent to the \(x\)-axis when \(f(x)=0\) **and** \(f'(x)=0\).

\[
f'(x)=35\pi\cos(7\pi\sin(5x))\cos(5x).
\]

At a zero, \(\sin(5x)=k/7\) so \(\cos(7\pi\sin(5x))=\cos(k\pi)=(-1)^k\neq0\).  
Hence \(f'(x)=0\) requires \(\cos(5x)=0\).

\[
\cos(5x)=0 \iff 5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+m\pi.
\]

For these points \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\), which means \(k=\pm7\).  
Thus only the solutions with \(k=\pm7\) are tangent.

Each of \(k=7\) and \(k=-7\) gives 5 solutions, so

\[
t=5+5=10.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Final sum

\[
n+t=139+10=149.
\]

\[
\boxed{149}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

* \(1\)  – a chair that is chosen,  
* \(0\) – an empty chair.  

A choice of eight chairs corresponds to a binary string of length \(16\) with exactly eight \(1\)’s.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  The restriction

“No person sits next to two other people’’ means that a chosen chair may **not** have occupied chairs on **both** sides.  
Hence a pattern \(111\) (three consecutive 1’s) cannot occur.

So we must count all binary strings of length \(16\) with eight \(1\)’s and **no** block \(111\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Represent the string as blocks

Because \(111\) is forbidden, every run of consecutive \(1\)’s has length either \(1\) or \(2\).

Let  

* \(a\) – number of runs of length \(2\) (\(11\)),  
* \(b\) – number of runs of length \(1\) (\(1\)).

We have

\[
2a+b=8 , \qquad a\ge 0,\; b\ge 0 .
\]

The total number of runs is

\[
k=a+b .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Distributing the zeros

The \(16\) positions contain \(8\) zeros.  
Between two consecutive runs there must be at least one zero, therefore the \(k-1\) internal gaps use \(k-1\) zeros.  
After that \(8-(k-1)=9-k\) zeros remain and can be placed arbitrarily among

\[
\underbrace{\;∗\;}_{\text{before first run}},\;
\underbrace{\;∗\;}_{\text{after first run}},\;
\ldots ,\;
\underbrace{\;∗\;}_{\text{after last run}}
\]

a total of \(k+1\) “gaps”.  
Stars‑and‑bars gives

\[
\binom{(9-k)+(k+1)-1}{k+1-1}
  =\binom{9}{k}
\]

ways to distribute the remaining zeros.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Choosing the order of runs

Among the \(k\) runs, choose which \(a\) of them have length \(2\); the rest are length \(1\).  
This can be done in

\[
\binom{k}{a}
\]

ways.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total for a specific \((a,b)\)

\[
\text{ways}_{(a,b)}=\binom{k}{a}\binom{9}{k},
\qquad k=a+b .
\]

Now enumerate the admissible \((a,b)\):

| \(a\) | \(b\) | \(k\) | \(\binom{k}{a}\) | \(\binom{9}{k}\) | Product |
|-------|-------|-------|-------------------|------------------|---------|
| 0 | 8 | 8 | 1 | 9 | 9 |
| 1 | 6 | 7 | 7 | 36 | 252 |
| 2 | 4 | 6 | 15 | 84 | 1260 |
| 3 | 2 | 5 | 10 | 126 | 1260 |
| 4 | 0 | 4 | 1 | 126 | 126 |

Adding all contributions,

\[
N = 9+252+1260+1260+126 = 2907 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
2907 \equiv 907 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{907}
\]

Results

Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be labelled \(0,1,\dots ,23\) in clockwise order.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Equal‑length segments  
In a regular polygon all chords having the same number of edges between their
end‑points have the same length.  
Hence if the 12 segments are of equal length, every chosen pair of vertices must
be a fixed distance \(k\;(1\le k\le 12)\) apart (if \(k=24-k\) we get the same
chord).  
So we have to count perfect matchings of the graph whose edges join vertices
\(i\) and \(i+k\pmod{24}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  The graph for a fixed \(k\)

For a fixed \(k\) let  

\[
E_k=\{\{i,i+k\}\mid i=0,1,\dots ,23\}.
\]

The graph \(G_k=(V,E_k)\) has degree \(2\) at each vertex, except in the
case \(k=12\) (diameters) where every vertex has degree \(1\).

The vertices of \(G_k\) split into cycles.
A step of \(k\) moves along the circle by \(k\) positions, so the orbit of a
vertex has length  

\[
L_k=\frac{24}{\gcd(24,k)} .
\]

Consequently

* if \(k=12\): the graph consists of \(12\) disjoint edges (components of size
  \(2\));  
* otherwise: the graph is a disjoint union of \(\displaystyle
   \frac{24}{L_k}\) cycles, each of length \(L_k\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Matchings inside a component

* For a cycle of length \(L\ge 4\) (even), there are exactly two perfect
  matchings (take alternate edges).
* For a component consisting of a single edge (\(L=2\)), there is only one
  perfect matching.
* A component of odd size cannot be perfectly matched, so if any component is
  odd, there are **no** matchings for that \(k\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Counting matchings for each \(k\)

| \(k\) | Component sizes | #components | #matchings |
|-------|-----------------|-------------|------------|
| 1 | 24 | 1 | \(2\) |
| 2 | 12,12 | 2 | \(2^2=4\) |
| 3 | 8,8,8 | 3 | \(2^3=8\) |
| 4 | 6,6,6,6 | 4 | \(2^4=16\) |
| 5 | 24 | 1 | \(2\) |
| 6 | 4,4,4,4,4,4 | 6 | \(2^6=64\) |
| 7 | 24 | 1 | \(2\) |
| 8 | 3,3,3,3,3,3,3,3 | 8 | **0** (odd components) |
| 9 | 8,8,8 | 3 | \(2^3=8\) |
|10 | 12,12 | 2 | \(2^2=4\) |
|11 | 24 | 1 | \(2\) |
|12 | twelve edges (size 2 each) | 12 | \(1^{12}=1\) |

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total number of admissible drawings

Sum of matchings for all admissible \(k\):

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+8+4+2+1=113 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{113}
\]

Let  

\[
d_k=|A_1A_k|\qquad (k=1,\ldots ,11),\qquad d_1=0 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Conditions on \(d_k\)

For every \(2\le i\le10\)

\[
\text{Area}(A_iA_1A_{i+1})=1,\qquad 
\cos\!\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13}.
\]

If \(\theta=\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}\), then \(\sin\theta=\frac{5}{13}\) (right triangle
\(12,5,13\)).  
Using the area formula for a triangle,

\[
\frac12 d_i d_{i+1}\sin\theta=1
    \Longrightarrow d_i d_{i+1}= \frac{2\cdot13}{5}=\frac{26}{5}\qquad(2\le i\le10).
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Edge lengths

For the side \(A_iA_{i+1}\;(2\le i\le10)\)

\[
A_iA_{i+1}^2
   =d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-2d_id_{i+1}\cos\theta
   =d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-2\cdot\frac{26}{5}\cdot\frac{12}{13}
   =d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2-\frac{48}{5}.
\]

With  
\(S_i=d_i+d_{i+1}\) we have

\[
d_i^2+d_{i+1}^2=S_i^2-2\cdot\frac{26}{5}.
\]

Thus

\[
A_iA_{i+1}^2 = S_i^2-\frac{48}{5}-\frac{52}{5}
            =S_i^2-20.
\]

So for \(2\le i\le10\)

\[
A_iA_{i+1}=\sqrt{(d_i+d_{i+1})^2-20}\; . \tag{2}
\]

The first and last side lengths are simply

\[
A_1A_2=d_2,\qquad A_{11}A_1=d_{11}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  The two distinct distances

From (1),

\[
d_2d_3=d_3d_4=\cdots =d_{10}d_{11}= \frac{26}{5}.
\]

Hence

\[
d_4=\frac{26/5}{d_3}=\frac{26/5}{\frac{26/5}{d_2}}=d_2,\\[2mm]
d_5=\frac{26/5}{d_4}=d_3,\qquad\text{etc.}
\]

So the distances from \(A_1\) to the vertices alternate:

\[
d_2=d_4=d_6=d_8=d_{10}=a,\qquad   
d_3=d_5=d_7=d_9=d_{11}=b,
\]
where  
\(a=d_2,\; b=d_3\) and \(ab=\dfrac{26}{5}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  The perimeter

Let  

\[
s=a+b .
\]

Every edge \(A_iA_{i+1}\;(2\le i\le10)\) uses one \(a\) and one \(b\),
hence by (2) its length is

\[
\sqrt{s^2-20}.
\]

There are nine such edges.  
The perimeter \(P\) is therefore

\[
P= a+b+9\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=s+9\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=20 .
\]

Solve for \(s\):

\[
9\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=20-s            
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad   
\sqrt{s^{2}-20}=\frac{20-s}{9}.
\]

Square both sides:

\[
s^{2}-20=\frac{(20-s)^{2}}{81}\;,
\]
\[
81(s^{2}-20)=(20-s)^{2},
\]
\[
80s^{2}+40s-2020=0.
\]

Divide by \(20\):

\[
4s^{2}+2s-101=0 .
\]

Using the quadratic formula,

\[
s=\frac{-2\pm\sqrt{4+1616}}{8}
   =\frac{-2\pm 18\sqrt5}{8}.
\]

Only the positive root fits \(s\le20\):

\[
s=\frac{-2+18\sqrt5}{8}
  =\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

Thus

\[
a+b=s=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}.
\]

so that \(AB=x,\ AC=y\) and  

\[
x^{2}+y^{2}=BC^{2}=38^{2}=1444 .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Position of \(K\) and \(L\)

Denote  

\[
K=(u,v), \; L=(s,t).
\]

The given distances give

\[
\begin{aligned}
&u^{2}+v^{2}=196          &&(AK=14),\\
&(u-x)^{2}+v^{2}=196      &&(BK=14),\\
&s^{2}+t^{2}=196          &&(AL=14),\\
&s^{2}+(t-y)^{2}=196      &&(CL=14),\\
&(u-s)^{2}+(v-t)^{2}=196  &&(KL=14).
\end{aligned}
\]

Subtract the second equation from the first:

\[
-2ux+x^{2}=0 \implies x=2u .
\tag{2}
\]

Subtract the fourth equation from the third:

\[
-2ty+y^{2}=0 \implies y=2t .
\tag{3}
\]

Hence  

\[
u=\frac{x}{2},\qquad v^{2}=196-\frac{x^{2}}{4},
\]
\[
s=\sqrt{196-\frac{y^{2}}{4}},\qquad t=\frac{y}{2}.
\]

Insert these into the final distance equation:

\[
\Bigl(\frac{x}{2}-s\Bigr)^{2}
   +\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{x^{2}}{4}}-\frac{y}{2}\Bigr)^{2}=196 .
\]

Together with (1) this system gives the solutions  
\[
\{x,y\}=\{26,\;16\sqrt3\}\quad\text{or}\quad \{16\sqrt3,\;26\}.
\]

Either case is acceptable because the triangle can be reflected over the
bisector of the right angle.  
We shall take  

\[
AB=26,\qquad AC=16\sqrt3 ,\qquad BC=38 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Coordinates of \(K\) and \(L\)

Using (2)–(3),

\[
K=\Bigl(\frac{26}{2},\ \sqrt{196-\frac{26^{2}}{4}}\Bigr)
  =(13,\;3\sqrt3),
\]
\[
L=\Bigl(\sqrt{196-\frac{(16\sqrt3)^{2}}{4}},\ \frac{16\sqrt3}{2}\Bigr)
  =\bigl(2,\ 8\sqrt3\bigr).
\]

One checks immediately that all required distances are \(14\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Area of \(BKLC\)

The vertices in order \(B\to K\to L\to C\) are

\[
B(26,0),\quad K(13,3\sqrt3),\quad
L(2,8\sqrt3),\quad C(0,16\sqrt3).
\]

Using the shoelace formula:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}&=\frac12\Bigl|
26(3\sqrt3)+13(8\sqrt3)+2(16\sqrt3)+0\cdot0 \\
&\quad-\bigl(
0\cdot13+3\sqrt3\cdot2+8\sqrt3\cdot0+16\sqrt3\cdot26\bigr)\Bigr|\\[4pt]
&=\frac12\bigl( (78+104+32)\sqrt3-(0+6\sqrt3+0+416\sqrt3) \bigr)\\[4pt]
&=104\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\) is \(104\sqrt3\), i.e.  
\(n=104\).

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}, \qquad x>0 ,
\]

and  

\[
f'(x)=0\Longleftrightarrow 
3x^4+(-2k-376)x^3+(188k+10116)x^2-127008k=0 .
\tag{1}
\]

Equation (1) is a quartic in \(x\); in order for \(f\) to have a minimum at two distinct
positive points it must possess exactly three positive critical points
(two of them being minima and the other a maximum).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Rewriting the derivative

\[
f'=\frac{N(x)}{x^2},\qquad 
N(x)=3x^4+(-2k-376)x^3+(188k+10116)x^2-127008k .
\]

The sign of \(f'\) is determined solely by \(N(x)\).
Because \(f(x)\to +\infty\) as \(x\to 0^+\) and as \(x\to +\infty\),
the graph of \(f\) goes up, then down, then up, up and finally
back to infinity when it has exactly two minima.

Thus \(N(x)\) must have *three* distinct **positive** real roots,
say

\[
r_1<r_2<r_3 ,
\]

with  

\[
f(r_1)=f(r_3)\quad (\text{global minima}) ,\qquad   
f(r_2)=\text{maximum}.
\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Solving the conditions

From \(N(r_i)=0\;(i=1,2,3)\) we get that each \(r_i\) satisfies

\[
3r_i^4+(-2k-376)r_i^3+(188k+10116)r_i^2-127008k=0 .
\tag{3}
\]

Because \(f(r_1)=f(r_3)\),

\[
\frac{(r_1-18)(r_1-72)(r_1-98)(r_1-k)}{r_1}
=\frac{(r_3-18)(r_3-72)(r_3-98)(r_3-k)}{r_3}.
\tag{4}
\]

Eliminating \(r_1,r_3\) from (3)–(4) gives a polynomial equation in \(k\);
the roots of this equation are exactly the admissible values of \(k\).

Carrying out the elimination (by symbolic computation or by a
numerical root‑finder over \(k>0\)) yields the three positive solutions  

\[
k=8,\qquad k=32,\qquad k=200 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Verification

For each value of \(k\) we can locate the three positive critical points
by solving (1):

| \(k\) | critical points \(x\) | \(f(x)\) |
|-------|-----------------------|----------|
| 8     | \(11.677,\;40.885,\;86.323\) | \(-10368,\;32711.8,\;-10368\) |
| 32    | \(23.235,\;50.084,\;86.765\) | \(-7200,\;12165.4,\;-7200\) |
| 200   | \(30.902,\;84.539,\;163.098\) | \(-194688,\;15338.9,\;-194688\) |

In every case the two outer critical points give the same
negative value, which is the global minimum of \(f\) on \((0,\infty)\).
Hence each of the three values \(k=8,32,200\) satisfies the required
condition.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Sum of the three admissible \(k\)

\[
k_1+k_2+k_3=8+32+200=240 .
\]

\[
\boxed{240}
\]
